11.4 Values of Trigonometric Functions

We know the values of trigonometric functions for angles of measure 0°, 30°, 45°, 60°, and 90°. We have also established the following identities:

sin (-θ) = -sin θ
cos (-θ) = cos θ
tan (-θ) = -tan θ
sin (π - θ) = sin θ
cos (π - θ) = -cos θ
tan (π - θ) = -tan θ
sin (π + θ) = -sin θ
cos (π + θ) = -cos θ
tan (π + θ) = tan θ
sin (2π - θ) = sin θ
cos (2π - θ) = cos θ
tan (2π - θ) = -tan θ

By using the above identities, we can easily find the values of trigonometric functions of the angles of the following measures:
-30°, -45°, -60°, -90°
±120°, ±135°, ±150°, ±180°
±210°, ±225°, ±240°, ±270°
±300°, ±315°, ±330°, ±360°

11.4.1 Graphs of Trigonometric Functions

To plot the graph we shall follow these steps:
(i) Table of ordered pairs (x, y) is constructed, when x is the measure of the angle and y is the value of the trigonometric function for the angle of measure x.
(ii) The measures of the angles are taken along the X-axis.
(iii) The values of the trigonometric functions are taken along the Y-axis.

--- PAGE 206 ---

Unit 11 Trigonometric Functions and their Graphs
Mathematics 11
286

(iv) The points corresponding to the ordered pairs are plotted on the graph paper.
(v) These points are joined with the help of smooth curves.

11.4.2 Graph of  y = sin x  from -2π to 2π

We know that the period of sine function is 2π so, we will first draw the graph for the interval from 0° to 360° (from 0 to 2π).

To graph the sine function, first, recall that -1 ≤ sin x ≤ 1 for all x ∈ ℝ.

We know the range of the sine function is [-1, 1], so the graph will be between the horizontal lines y = +1 and y = -1.

The table of the ordered pairs satisfying y = sin x is as follows:

| x | 0 | (π)/6 | (π)/3 | (π)/2 | (2π)/3 | (5π)/6 | π | (7π)/6 | (4π)/3 | (5π)/3 | (11π)/6 | 2π | ------------------------or------------------------------------------------------

| 0° | 30° | 60° | 90° | 120° | 150° | 180° | 210° | 240° | 270° | 300° | 360° |

| sin x | 0 | 0.5 | 0.87 | 1 | 0.87 | 0.5 | 0 | -0.5 | -0.87 | -1 | -0.87 | 0 |

To draw the graph:
(i) Take a convenient scale . side of small square on the x-axis = 10° . side of big square on the y-axis = 1 unit
(ii) Draw the coordinate axes.
(iii) Plot the points corresponding to the ordered pairs in the table above i.e., (0, 0), (30°, 0.5), (60°, 0.87) and so on.
(iv) Join the points with the help of a smooth curve as shown. So, we get the graph of y = sin x from 0 to 360° i.e., from 0 to 2π.

Note As we see that the graphs of trigonometric functions are smooth curves and none of them is line segment or has sharp corners or breaks within their domain. This behaviour of the curve is called continuity. It means that the trigonometric functions are continuous, wherever they are defined. Moreover, as the trigonometric functions are periodic so their curves repeat after fixed intervals.

In the similar way, we can draw the graph for the interval from 0° to -360°. This will complete the graph of y = sin x from -360° to 360° (from -2π to 2π), which is given below:

Graph of y = sin x from -360° to 360°

The graph in the interval [0, 2π] is called a cycle and the maximum height of the wave from its mid line is called amplitude. Since the period of sine function is 2π, so the sine graph can be extended on both sides of x-axis through every interval of 2π.

Properties of graph of sine function (y = sin x)

(i) The domain is the set of real numbers (-∞ < x < ∞).

(ii) The range includes all real numbers from -1 to 1, inclusive, [-1, 1].

(iii) The graph of sine function is continuous for all real numbers.

(iv) The period of sine function is 2π. Mathematically, we can express it as sin(θ + 2π) = sinθ.

(v) The sine function is an odd function. As the graph of sine function is symmetric about the origin, Mathematically, it can be written as sin(-θ) = -sinθ.

(vi) The maximum value of y = sin x is 1 when x = (π)/2 + 2π n, where n ∈ Z.

(vii) The minimum value of y = sin x is -1 when x = (3π)/2 + 2π n, where n ∈ Z.

(viii) The x-intercepts of the sine function occurs at x = π n, where n ∈ Z.

(ix) The y-intercept of the sine function is 0.

(x) The amplitude of sine function is 1.

(xi) In unit circle sinθ is equal to the y-coordinate of the given point.

(ii) The range includes all real numbers from -1 to 1, inclusive, [-1, 1].

(iii) The graph of cosine function is continuous for all real numbers.

(iv) The period of cosine function is 2π. Mathematically, we can express it as cos(θ + 2π) = cosθ.

11.4.4 Graph of y=tan x from −π to π

We know that tan (−x)=−tan x and tan (π−x)=−tan x, so the values of tan x for x=0°, 30°, 60°, 90° can help us in making the table.
Also, we know that tan x is undefined at x=±90°, when

(i) x approaches π/2 from left x→(π/2), tan x decreases indefinitely in Quard I.
(ii) x approaches π/2 from right i.e., x→(π/2), tan x decreases indefinitely in Quard IV.
(iii) x approaches −π/2 from left i.e., x→(−π/2), tan x increases indefinitely in Quard II.
(iv) x approaches −π/2 from right i.e., x→(−π/2), tan x decreases indefinitely in Quard III.

We know that the period of tangent is π, so we shall first draw the graph for the interval from 0 to π (from 0° to 180°).

11.5 Maximum and Minimum Values of Given Functions of the Type
-  a + b sin θ
-  a + b cos θ
-  a + b sin (cθ + d)
-  a + b cos (cθ + d)

The reciprocals of the above, where  a ,  b ,  c , and  d  are real numbers,

The trigonometric functions like sine and cosine are periodic functions because the values of these functions repeat over regular intervals. These functions are fundamental in mathematics because of the repetition of their values at definite cycles and are used to model various real-life situations, such as radio waves, light waves, and alternating current in electricity and are also known as a specific case of sinusoidal functions.

The functions of the form  f(θ) = a + b sin θ ,  g(θ) = a + b cos θ ,  f₁(θ) = a + b sin (cθ + d)  and  g₁(θ) = a + b cos (cθ + d)  are the types of sinusoidal functions.

Now consider the general form of sinusoidal function  f₁(θ) = a + b sin (cθ + d)  ... (i)
here 'a' represents the vertical shift refers to the vertical translation of the graph of the function, achieved by shifting the entire graph upward or downward. This shift, also known as the vertical displacement, moves the function's position along the y-axis without altering its shape or period. Amplitude  |b|  is the maximum height of a wave

--- PAGE 212 ---

Unit 11 Trigonometric Functions and their Graphs
Mathematics 11

measured from its midline. The period of (i) is equal to  (2π)/c . Phase shift 'd' indicates the horizontal translation of the graph of the function, determining how far the wave is shifted left or right along the x-axis. A positive  d  shifts the graph to the left, while a negative  d  shifts it to the right, altering the starting point of the wave without changing its shape or period.

For Example, consider the function  f(θ) = 1 + 3 sin(2θ) . Here  a = 1  is vertical shift, amplitude  = |b| = |3| = 3  and period  = (2π)/2 = π  as shown in the adjacent figure.

Now, finding the maximum and minimum values of the functions  f(θ) = a + b sin(cθ + d)  and  g(θ) = a + b cos(cθ + d)  is not a difficult task. We know that the maximum absolute values of sine and cosine are equal to 1, so the maximum value of the product  b sinθ  is  |b| .

Thus, the maximum value of  f(θ)  or  g(θ)  is :  M = a + |b| , whenever  sinθ = 1  or  cosθ = 1  where  M  denotes the maximum value of the function.

The minimum value of  f(θ)  or  g(θ)  function is  m = a - |b| , whenever  sinθ = -1  or  cosθ = -1  and  m  denotes the minimum value of the function.

Note: The absolute value of  b  is called the Amplitude of  f(θ) = a + b sinθ . The value of the amplitude can also be determined using the formula
Amplitude  = (Maximum value - Minimum value)/2

Example 2 Find the maximum and minimum values of the following functions:
(i)  2 + 3 sin x
(ii)  5 - 2 cos 3x
(iii) reciprocal of (ii)

Solution (i) Let  f(x) = 2 + 3 sin x

The maximum value of  f(x)  will occur when  sin x = 1 . Here  a = 2  and  b = 3 ,
Maximum value of the function:  M = a + |b| = 2 + 3 = 5

The minimum value of the function will occur when  sin x = -1 .
Minimum value of the function:  m = a - |b| = 2 - 3 = -1

Thus, maximum value of the function is 5 and the minimum value is -1

--- PAGE 213 ---

Unit 11 Trigonometric Functions and their Graphs
Mathematics 11
Page 213

(ii) Let  f(x) = 5 - 2 cos 3x

The maximum value of  f(x)  will occur when  cos 3x = 1 . Here  a = 5  and  b = -2 .
Maximum value of the function:  M = a + |b| = 5 + |-2| = 5 + 2 = 7 .
The minimum value of the function will occur when  cos 3x = -1 .
Minimum value of the function:  m = a - |b| = 5 - |-2| = 5 - 2 = 3 .
Thus, the maximum value of the function is 7 and the minimum value is 3.

(iii) Reciprocal of part (ii)

The reciprocal of  5 - 2 cos 3x  is  1/(5 - 2 cos 3x) .

Let  g(x) = 1/(5 - 2 cos 3x)

To find the maximum and minimum values of  g(x) , first we will find the maximum and minimum values of  5 - 2 cos 3x , which are 7 and 3 respectively.
After finding the maximum and minimum values take their reciprocal. The reciprocal of the maximum value is the minimum of  g(x)  and the reciprocal of the minimum value is the maximum of  g(x) .

Maximum value of  g(x) = 1/m = 1/3 = 0.33
Minimum value of  g(x) = 1/M = 1/7 = 0.14

### Real World Applications

#### Ferris Wheel Problems

The first Ferris wheel was invented by George W. Ferris. He built the first one for the 1893 World's Fair. A Ferris wheel is an important example of periodic motion that can be described using trigonometric functions, specifically sinusoidal functions. When we model the height of a rider on a Ferris wheel over time, we can use these functions to capture the periodic nature of the motion. The motion of a Ferris wheel can be modeled by  f(t) = a + b sin(ct + d)  or  f(t) = a + b cos(ct + d) .

**Example 3:** A Ferris wheel with a radius of 45 feet has its lowest point located 5 feet above the ground. It completes one full revolution every 60 seconds in a counter-clockwise direction. Model an equation that describes the height of a rider on the Ferris wheel as a function of time  t . How high is the rider from the ground after 40 seconds? Also graph the model equation.

**Solution:** Since it takes 60 seconds for the Ferris wheel to complete one full revolution

--- PAGE 214 ---

Unit 11 Trigonometric Functions and their Graphs
Mathematics 11
214

(one cycle), which is the period of the Ferris wheel, that is period = 60
 (2π)/c = 60 ⇒ c = (2π)/60 ⇒ c = (π)/30

The amplitude  b  which is equal to the radius of a ferris wheel (in this case  b = 45 ).
The vertical shift  a  is the height of the center of the Ferris wheel above the ground.
Since the lowest point is 5 feet above the ground, so  a = 5 + b = 5 + 45 = 50 .
We can model the height of a rider using (sine or cosine), because it reflects the periodic nature of the motion. We usually choose a cosine function if the rider starts at the maximum or minimum height, or a sine function if the rider starts at the midpoint.
Since the rider starts at the lowest point and goes up, we can easily model the required equation as a negative cosine function so,
 h(t) = -bcos(α t) + a , where  t  is time and  h  is height.

Now substituting the above values we get the function  h(t) = -45cos((π/30)t) + 50 ,
which is the required equation of Ferris wheel.
Next, we find the height of the rider at  t = 40  seconds,
 h(t) = -45cos((π/30)t) + 50
For  t = 40 , we have
 h(40) = -45cos((π)/30 .40) + 50 = 72.5 feet

Thus, height of rider after 40 second is 72.5 feet.
The graph of the model equation is shown below.

Example 4 The water level L (in feet) of a tidal river varies throughout the day. Suppose the level of the tidal river can be modeled by the equation: L(t)=8+4sin ((π/6)t), where t denotes the time (in hours). The water level oscillates 4 feet above and below an average level of 8 feet.

(a) Find the water level at t=3 hours?

(b) What is the minimum water level?

Solution
(a) Given equation of water level: L(t)=8+4sin ((π/6)t)

To find the water level, substitute t=3 into the equation
L(3)=8+4sin ((π/6)·3)=8+4sin ((π)/2)
L(3)=8+4(1)=12

Thus, water level at t=3 hours is 12 feet.

(b) Now, to find the minimum water level, we need to determine when the sine function attains its minimum value. We know that the minimum value of sin t=-1, substitute the sin ((π)/6t)=-1 into the equation
L(t)=8+4sin ((π/6)t)=8+4(-1)=8-4=4

Thus, minimum water level of the tidal river is 4 feet.

Example 5 From a point 100 m above the surface of a lake, the angle of elevation of a peak of a cliff is found to be 15° and the angle of depression of the image of the peak is 30°. Find the height of the peak.

Solution Let A be the top of the peak (AM) and (MB) be its image. Let P be the point of observation and L be the point just below P (on the surface of the lake).

From P, draw (PQ) ⊥ (AM).

Let m(PQ)=y metres and m(AM)=h metres.

∴    m(AQ)=h-m(QM)=h-m(PL)=h-100


From the figure,

tan 15°=AQ/PQ=(h-100)/y    and    tan 30°=BQ/PQ=(100+h)/y

--- PAGE 216 ---

Mathematics 11
Page 216

By division, we get
tan 15°/tan 30° = h - 100 /h + 100

By Componendo and Dividendo, we have
tan 15° + tan 30° / tan 15° - tan 30° = h - 100 + h + 100 h - 100 - h - 100 
= 2h/-200 = h/-100

∴ h = tan 30° + tan 15° / tan 30° - tan 15° tand × 100 = [0.5774 + 0.2679] × 100
⇒ h = 273.1179.

Hence height of the peak = 273 m. (approximately)

Unit 12
Limit and Continuity

INTRODUCTION
In mathematics, the concept of limit and continuity is foundational in understanding the behaviour of functions and sequences, especially when applied to real-world scenarios. This unit will introduce and explore how to demonstrate and find the limit of a sequence and a function, understand continuous and discontinuous functions, and apply these concepts in various contexts such as economics, finance, and natural sciences.

12.1 Limit of a Function
The concept of limit of a function is the basis on which the structure of calculus rests. Before the definition of the limit of a function, it is necessary to have a clear understanding of the following phrases.

12.1.1 Meaning of the Phrase “x approaches zero”
Suppose a sequence  xₙ = 1/(n²)  assumes a sequence of values as:
1, 1/2, 1/(2²), 1/(2³), 1/(2⁴), ..., 1/(2ⁿ), ...

We can see that the sequence  xₙ = 1/(n²)  is becoming smaller and smaller as  n  increases and can be made as small as we please by taking "n" sufficiently large. In other words,  xₙ = 1/(n²)  becoming closer and closer to 0 as  n  becoming large. This unending decrease of  xₙ  is denoted by  xₙ → 0  and read as “ xₙ  approaches zero” or “ xₙ  tends to zero as  n → ∞ . That is, the limit of the sequence  xₙ  is 0.

12.1.2 Meaning of the Phrase “x approaches infinity”
Suppose a sequence  xₙ = 10ⁿ  assumes values as 1, 10,  10² ,  10³ , ...,  10ⁿ , ...

It is clear that the sequence  xₙ  is becoming larger and larger as  n  increases and can be made as large as we please by taking  n  sufficiently large. This unending increase of the sequence  xₙ  is symbolically written as “ xₙ → ∞ ” and is read as “ xₙ  approaches infinity” or “ xₙ  tends to infinity” as  n → ∞ .

--- PAGE 219 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
219 Mathematics 11

12.1.3 Meaning of the Phrase "x approaches a"
Symbolically it is written as "x→a" which means that x is sufficiently close to a but different from the number a, from both the left and right sides of a that is x−a becomes smaller and smaller as we please but x−a≠0.

Point to remember:
The symbol x→0 is quite different from x=0.
x→0 means that x is very close to zero but not actually zero.
x=0 means that x is actually zero.

12.1.4 Concept of Limit of a Function
(I) By Finding the Area of Circumscribed Regular Polygon
Consider a circle of unit radius which circumscribes a square (4-sided regular polygon) as shown in Figure 12.1.

The side of square is √2 and its area is 2 square units. It is clear that the area of inscribed 4-sided polygon is less than the area of circum-circle π=3.142(πr²=π(1)²=π=3.142).

Figure 12.1: 4-sided polygon
Figure 12.2: 8-sided polygon
Figure 12.3: 16-sided polygon

Bisecting the arcs between the vertices of the square, we get an inscribed 8-sided regular polygon as shown in Figure 12.2. Its area is 2√2=2.828 square units which is closer to the area of circum-circle. A further similar bisection of the arcs gives an inscribed 16-sided regular polygon as shown in Figure 12.3 with area 3.061 square units which is more closer to the area of circum-circle.

It follows that as “n”, the number of sides of the inscribed polygon increases, the area of polygon increases and becoming near to 3.142 which is the area of circle of unit radius.

We express this situation by saying that the limiting value of the area of the inscribed polygon is the area of the circle as n approaches infinity, i.e.,

Area of inscribed polygon → Area of circle as n →∞

--- PAGE 220 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
Mathematics 11

(II) Numerical Approach

Consider the function  f(x) = x³

The domain of  f(x)  is the set of all real numbers.

Let us find the limit of  f(x) = x³  as  x  approaches 2.

The table of values of  f(x)  for different values of  x  as  x  approaches 2 from left and right is as follows:

| From left of 2 | 2 | From right of 2 |


| x | 1 | 1.5 | 1.8 | 1.9 | 1.99 | 1.999 | 1.9999 | 2.0001 | 2.001 | 2.01 | 2.1 | 2.2 | 2.5 | 3 |

|f(x) = x³ | 1 | 3.375 | 5.832 | 6.859 | 7.8806 | 7.8806 | 7.9988 | 8.0012 | 8.012 | 8.1206 | 9.261 | 10.648 | 15.625 | 27 |

The table shows that, as  x  gets closer and closer to 2 (sufficiently close to 2), from both sides,  f(x)  gets closer and closer to 8.

We say that 8 is the limit of  f(x)  when  x  approaches 2 and is written as:

 f(x) → 8  as  x → 2  or  lim_(x → 2) x³ = 8

12.1.5 Limit of a Function

Let a function  f(x)  be defined in an open interval near the number "a" (need not be at  a ). If, as  x  approaches "a" from both left and right side of "a",  f(x)  approaches a specific number "L", then "L" is called the limit of  f(x)  as  x  approaches  a . Symbolically it is written as:

 lim_(x → a) f(x) = L  read as "limit of  f(x)  as  x → a , is  L ".

It is neither desirable nor practicable to find the limit of a function by numerical approach. We must be able to evaluate a limit in some mechanical way. The theorems on limits will serve this purpose. Their proofs will be discussed in higher classes.

12.1.6 Theorems on Limits of Functions

Let  f  and  g  be two functions for which  lim_(x → a) f(x) = L  and  lim_(x → a) g(x) = M , then

Theorem 1:
(i)  lim_(x → a) x^(p) = a^(p) , where  p > 0  and  p ∈ ℝ
(ii)  lim_(x → a) c = c

Theorem 2: (a) The limit of the sum of two functions is equal to the sum of their limits.

 lim_(x → a) [f(x) + g(x)] = lim_(x → a) f(x) + lim_(x → a) g(x) = L + M

For example,  lim_(x → 1) (x + 5) = lim_(x → 1) x + lim_(x → 1) 5 = 1 + 5 = 6

--- PAGE 221 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
Mathematics 11

(b) The limit of the difference of two functions is equal to the difference of their limits.
Lim_(x->a)[f(x) - g(x)] = Lim_(x->a) f(x) - Lim_(x->a) ( g(x) = L - M
For example, Lim_(x->3) (x - 5) = Lim_(x->3) (x) - Lim_(x->3) 5 = 3 - 5 = -2

(c) If k is any real number, then
Lim_(x->a) [kf(x)] = k Lim_(x->a) f(x) = kL
For example, Lim_(x->2)(3x) = 3 Lim(x->2)(x) = 3(2) = 6

(d) The limit of the product of the functions is equal to the product of their limits.
Lim_(x->a)[f(x)g(x)] = Lim_(x->a) f(x) · Lim_(x->a) g(x) = LM
For example, Lim_(x->1) (2x)(x + 4) = Lim_(x->1) (2x) · Lim_(x->1) (x + 4) = (2)(5) = 10

(e) The limit of the quotient of the functions is equal to the quotient of their limits provided the limit of denominator is non-zero.
Lim_(x->a) [f(x)/g(x)] = Lim_(x->a) f(x) / Lim_(x->a) g(x) = L / M, provided g(x) ≠ 0 in a neighborhood of a and M ≠ 0
For example: Lim_(x->2) [(3x + 4)/(x + 3)] = Lim_(x->2) (3x + 4) / Lim_(x->2) (x + 3) = (6 + 4) / (2 + 3) = 10 / 5 = 2

(f) Limit of [f(x)]ⁿ, where n is an integer
Lim_(x->a) [f(x)]ⁿ = [Lim_(x->a) f(x)]ⁿ = Lⁿ
For example, Lim_(x->4) (2x - 3)³ = (Lim_(x->4) (2x - 3))³ = (5)³ = 125

We conclude from the theorems on limits that limits are evaluated by merely substituting the number that x approaches into the function.

### 12.2 Limits of Important Functions

If by substituting the number that  x  approaches into the function, we get  (0/0) , then one possible way to evaluate the limits is as follows:

We simplify the given function by using algebraic techniques of making factors if possible and cancel the common factors. The method explained in the following important limits.

#### 12.2.1 lim_(x → a) (xⁿ - aⁿ)/(x - a) = naⁿ⁻¹ where  n  is a non-zero integer and  a > 0

**Case I:** Suppose  n  is a positive integer.

By substituting  x = a , we get  (0/0)  form, so we make factors as follows:


xⁿ - aⁿ = (x-a)(xⁿ⁻¹ + axⁿ⁻² + a²xⁿ⁻³ + … + aⁿ⁻¹)


lim_(x → a) (xⁿ - aⁿ)/(x - a) = lim_(x → a) (x-a)(xⁿ⁻¹ + axⁿ⁻² + a²xⁿ⁻³ + … + aⁿ⁻¹)/(x-a)


= lim_(x → a) (xⁿ⁻¹ + axⁿ⁻² + a²xⁿ⁻³ + … + aⁿ⁻¹)


= aⁿ⁻¹ + a · aⁿ⁻² + a² · aⁿ⁻³ + … + aⁿ⁻¹ = naⁿ⁻¹.


**Case II:** Suppose  n  is a negative integer (Say  n = -m  where  m  is a positive integer).

Now,


(xⁿ - aⁿ)/(x - a) = (x^(-m) - a^(-m))/(x - a) = (1/(x^(m)) - 1/(a^(m)))/(x - a) = ((a^(m) - x^(m))/(x^(m) a^(m)))/(x - a)


∴    lim_(x → a) (xⁿ - aⁿ)/(x - a) = lim_(x → a) ( -1/(x^(m) a^(m)) ) ( (x^(m) - a^(m))/(x - a) )


= -1/(a^(m)*a^(m)) · ma^(m-1) = -ma^(-m-1).


∴    lim_(x → a) ( (xⁿ - aⁿ)/(x - a) ) = naⁿ⁻¹.

--- PAGE 223 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
223 Mathematics 11

12.2.2 Lim x→0 (√x+a−√a)/x = 1/2√a, where a is an integer and a > 0.

By substituting x = 0, we have (0/0) form, so rationalizing the numerator.

Lim x→0 √x+a−√a/x = Lim x→0 (√x+a−√a/x)(√x+a+√a) = Lim x→0 x+a−a/x(√x+a+√a)
= Lim x→0 1/(√x+a+√a) = 1/(√a+√a) = 1/2√a

Example 1 Evaluate: (i) Lim x→1 x²−1/x²−x (ii) Lim x→3 x−3/√x−√3

Solution (i) Lim x→1 x²−1/x²−x = (0/0) form.
Lim x→1 x²−1 = Lim x→1 (x−1)(x+1)/x(x−1) = Lim x→1 x+1/x
= 1+1/1 = 2/1 = 2

(ii) Lim x→3 x−3/√x−√3 = Lim x→3 (√x+√3)(√x−√3)/√x−√3 = Lim x→3 (√x+√3) = √3+√3 = 2√3

12.2.3 Limit at Infinity

We have studied the limits of the functions f(x), f(x), g(x) and f(x)/g(x), when x→c(a number).

Let us see what happens to the limit of the function f(x) if c is +∞ or −∞ (limits at infinity) i.e., when x→+∞ or x→−∞.

(a) Limit as x→+∞

Let f(x) = 1/x, when x≠0

This function has the property that the value of f(x) can be made as close as we please to zero when the number x is sufficiently large.

We express this phenomenon by writing Lim x→+∞ 1/x = 0

--- PAGE 224 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
224 Mathematics 11

(b) Limit as  x → -∞

This type of limits are handled in the same way as limits as  x → +∞ .

i.e.,  lim_(x → -∞) 1/x = 0 , where  x ≠ 0 .

The following theorem is useful for evaluating limit at infinity.

Theorem: Let  p  be a positive rational number. If  x^(p)  is defined, then

 lim_(x → +∞) a/(x^(p)) = 0  and  lim_(x → -∞) a/(x^(p)) = 0 , where  a  is any real number.

For example,  lim_(x → +∞) 6/(x³) = 0  and  lim_(x → +∞) -5/(sqrt[5]x) = 0 .

12.2.4 Limit of a Sequence

Let  aₙ  be a sequence, the limit of a sequence  aₙ  is the value  L  that the terms of the sequence approach as  n → ∞ , that is,

 lim_(n → ∞) aₙ = L

If such an  L  exists, the sequence is said to converge to  L  and  aₙ  is called convergent sequence. If no such  L  exists, the sequence is said to diverge.

For example, consider the sequence  aₙ = 1/n . As  n → ∞ ,  1/n → 0 .

So, we write  lim_(n → ∞) aₙ = lim_(n → ∞) 1/n = 0 .

Example 7 Find the limit of the sequence  aₙ = (2n+3)/(n+1) .

Solution We can simplify the sequence:

 aₙ = (2n+3)/(n+1) = (n((2+3/n)/n))/(n((1+1/n)/n)) = (2+3/n)/(1+1/n)

As  n → ∞ ,  3/n → 0  and  1/n → 0 , so we are left with:  lim_(n → ∞) aₙ = (2+0)/(1+0) = 2

Thus,  lim_(n → ∞) (2n+3)/(n+1) = 2

--- PAGE 225 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
225 Mathematics 11

Divergent Sequences: A sequence is divergent if it is not convergent. Divergence can occur in the following ways:
- The sequence may increase or decrease without bound (e.g., aₙ = n² diverges to infinity).
- The sequence may oscillate between different values and not settle near any one value (e.g., aₙ = (-1)ⁿ oscillates between -1 and 1, so it does not converge).

12.2.5 Methods for Evaluating the Limits at Infinity

In this case, we first divide each term of both the numerator and the denominator by the highest power of x that appears in the denominator and then use the theorems on limit.

Example 3 Evaluate lim_(x → +∞) (5x⁴ - 10x² + 1)/(-3x³ + 10x² + 50)

Solution Dividing numerator and denominator by x³, we get


lim_(x → +∞) (5x⁴ - 10x² + 1)/(-3x³ + 10x² + 50) = lim_(x → +∞) (5x - 10/x + 1/(x³))/(-3 + 10 · 1/x + 50 · 1/(x³)) = (∞ - 0 + 0)/(-3 + 0 + 0) = -∞ ∴ lim_(x → +∞) x/(x³) = 0


Example 4 Evaluate lim_(x → -∞) (4x⁴ - 5x³)/(-3x⁵ + 2x² + 1)

Solution Dividing numerator and denominator by x⁵, we get


lim_(x → -∞) (4x⁴ - 5x³)/(-3x⁵ + 2x² + 1) = lim_(x → -∞) (4/x - 5/(x²))/(-3 + 2/(x³) + 1/(x⁵)) = (0 - 0)/(-3 + 0 + 0) = 0


Example 5 Evaluate:

(i) lim_(x → +∞) (2 - 3x)/(√(3 + 4x²))

(ii) lim_(x → +∞) (2 - 3x)/(√(3 + 4x²))

Solution (i) Here √(x²) = |x| = -x as x < 0


∴    Dividing numerator and denominator by -x, we get


lim_(x → +∞) (2 - 3x)/(√(3 + 4x²)) = lim_(x → +∞) (-2/x + 3)/(√(3/(x²) + 4)) = (0 + 3)/(√(0 + 4)) = 3/2

(ii) Here √x² = |x| = x as x > 0
∴ Dividing numerator and denominator by x, we get
Lim x→∞ 2−3x / sqrt(3+4x^2) = Lim x→∞ (2/x -3)/sqrt(3/x^2 +4) = 0−3/sqrt(0+4) = −3

12.2.6 Lim (1+ 1)n = e
	n→+∞

(1+ 1/n)^n = 1+n(1/n)+ (n(n−1))/2!( 1/n)^2 + (n(n−1)(n−2))/3!( 1/n)^3 +...


When n → +∞, 1/n , 2/n , 3/n , ... all tends to zero, therefore

∴ Lim    (1+ 1/n)^n =1+1+ 1+ 1+ 1 ..
    x→+∞                  2! 3! 4!

=1+1+0.5+0.166667+0.0416667+...=2.718281...

As approximate value of e is 2.718281.

∴ Lim (1+ 1/n)^n =e
   n→+∞

12.2.7 Lim_(x->0) (aˣ - 1)/x = logₑ a

Put aˣ - 1 = y
then aˣ = 1 + y
So, x = logₐ(1 + y)

= lim_(y → 0) 1/(logₐ(1 + y)^(1/y)) = 1/(logₐ e) (lim_(y → 0) (1 + y)^(1/y) = e)

We know that lim_(x → 0) ((aˣ - 1)/x) = logₐ a
Put a = e in (i) we know lim_(x → 0) ((eˣ - 1)/x) = logₑ e = 1

Important Results to Remember

(ii) lim_(x → ∞) eˣ = lim_(x → ∞) (1/(eˣ)) = 0

12.2.8 The Sandwich Theorem
Let f, g and h be functions such that f(x)≤g(x)≤h(x) for all numbers x in some open interval containing "c", except possibly at c itself.
If Lim_x->e f(x)=L and Lim_x->e h(x)=L, then Lim_x->e g(x)=L
Many limit problems arise that cannot be directly evaluated by algebraic techniques. They require geometric arguments, so we evaluate an important theorem.

12.2.9 If θ is measured in radian, then Lim_θ->0 sin θ/θ=1.
Proof: To evaluate this limit, we apply a new technique. Take θ be positive acute central angle of a sector of a circle with radius r=1. As shown in the figure, OAB represents a sector of a circle. Join A and B and extend OB to D such that OA⊥AD. Also draw BC⊥OC on OA.
Given |OA|=|OB|=1 (radii of unit circle)
In the right ΔOBC, sin θ=|BC|/|OB|=|BC|
In the right ΔOAD, tan θ=|AD|/|OA|=|AD|
(i) Area of ΔOAB=1/2|OA||BC|=1/2(1)(sin θ)=1/2 sin θ
(ii) Area of sector OAB=1/2r²θ=1/2(1)(θ)=1/2θ and
(iii) Area of ΔOAD=1/2|OA||AD|=1/2(1)(tan θ)=1/2tan θ
From the figure we see that
Area of ΔOAB<Area of sector OAB<Area of ΔOAD
⇒ 1/2 sin θ < θ/2 < 1/2 tan θ

Figure 12.4

--- PAGE 229 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
Mathematics 11
229

As sin θ is positive, so on division by 1/2 sin θ, we get
 1 < (θ)/(sin θ) < 1/(cos θ)    (0 < θ < (π)/2)
i.e., 1 > (sin θ)/(θ) > cos θ or cos θ < (sin θ)/(θ) < 1

when θ → 0, cos θ → 1

Since (sin θ)/(θ) is sandwiched between 1 and a quantity approaching 1 itself. So, by the sandwich theorem, it must also approach 1 that is, lim_(θ → 0) (sin θ)/(θ) = 1

Note
The same result holds for -(π)/2 < θ < 0

Example 7 Evaluate lim_(θ → 0) (sin 7θ)/(θ)

Solution Let x = 7θ, so that θ = x/7

when θ → 0 we have x → 0

∴ lim_(θ → 0) (sin 7θ)/(θ) = lim_(x → 0) (sin x)/x/7 = 7 lim_(x → 0) (sin x)/x = (7)(1) = 7

Example 8 Evaluate lim_(θ → 0) (1 - cos θ)/(θ)

Solution (1 - cos θ)/(θ) = (1 - cos θ)/(θ) · (1 + cos θ)/(1 + cos θ) = (1 - cos² θ)/(θ(1 + cos θ))

= (sin² θ)/(θ(1 + cos θ)) = sin θ ((sin θ)/(θ)) (1/(1 + cos θ))

lim_(θ → 0) ((1 - cos θ)/(θ)) = lim_(θ → 0) sin θ · lim_(θ → 0) (sin θ)/(θ) · lim_(θ → 0) (1/(1 + cos θ)) = (0)(1)(1/(1 + 1)) = 0

12.3 Continuity and Discontinuity of Functions
12.3.1 One-Sided Limits

In defining Lim f(x), we restricted x in an open interval containing c i.e., we studied the behaviour of f on both sides of c. However, in some cases it is necessary to investigate one sided limits that is, the left hand limit and the right hand limit.

(i) The Left Hand Limit
Lim f(x)=L is read as the limit of f(x) is equal to L as x approaches c from the left i.e., for all x sufficiently close to c, but less than c, the value of f(x) can be made as close as we please to L.

(ii) The Right Hand Limit
Lim f(x)=M is read as the limit of f(x) is equal to M as x approaches c from the right i.e., for all x sufficiently close to c, but greater than c, the value of f(x) can be made as close as we please to M.

Note The rules for calculating the left hand and the right hand limits are the same as we studied to calculate limits in the preceding section.

12.3.2 Criterion for Existence of Limit of a Function
Lim_x->c f(x)=L if and only if Lim x->c^- f(x)=Lim_x->c^+ f(x)=L

Example 9 Determine whether Lim_x->2 f(x) and Lim_x->4 f(x) exist, when
f(x)=
{
2x+1 if 0≤x≤2
7-x if 2<x<4
x if 4≤x≤6
}

Solution (i) Lim_x->2^- f(x)=Lim_x->2^- (2x+1)=4+1=5
Lim_x->2^+ f(x)=Lim_x->2^+ (7-x)=7-2=5
Since Lim_x->2^- f(x)=Lim_x->2^+  f(x)=5
∴ Lim_x->2^+  f(x) exists and is equal to 5.

(ii) Lim_x->4^- f(x)=Lim_x->4^- (7-x)=7-4=3
Lim_x->4^+ f(x)=Lim_x->4^+ (x)=4
Since Lim_x->4^- f(x)≠Lim_x->4^+ f(x)
Therefore, Lim_x->4^+ f(x) does not exist.

Remember!
lim 1/x = -∞
lim 1/x = +∞

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
232 Mathematics 11

12.3.3 Continuity of a Function at a Point
(a) Continuous Function
A function  f  is said to be continuous at a number "c" if and only if the following three conditions are satisfied.
(i)  f(c)  is defined
(ii)  lim_(x → c) f(x)  exists
(iii)  lim_(x → c) f(x) = f(c)

(b) Discontinuous Function
If one or more of these three conditions fail to hold at "c", then the function  f  is said to be discontinuous at "c".

Example 10 Consider the function  f(x) = (x² - 1)/(x - 1) , discuss the continuity of  f  at  x = 1 .
Solution Here  f(1)  is not defined.
 ⇒ f(x)  is discontinuous at 1.

Example 11 For  f(x) = 3x² - 5x + 4 , discuss continuity of  f  at  x = 1 .
Solution  lim_(x → 1) f(x) = lim_(x → 1) (3x² - 5x + 4) = 3 - 5 + 4 = 2  and  f(1) = 3 - 5 + 4 = 2
 ⇒ lim_(x → 1) f(x) = f(1)
Therefore,  f(x)  is continuous at  x = 1 .

Example 12 Discuss the continuity of the functions  f(x)  and  g(x)  at  x = 3
(a)  f(x) =  (x² - 9)/(x - 3)   if x ≠ 3
 6   if x = 3
(b)  g(x) =  (x² - 9)/(x - 3)   if x ≠ 3
Solution (a)  f(3) = 6
Now,  lim_(x → 3) f(x) = lim_(x → 3) (x² - 9)/(x - 3)
 = lim_(x → 3) (x + 3)(x - 3)/(x - 3)
 = lim_(x → 3) (x + 3) = 3 + 3 = 6
As  lim_(x → 3) f(x) = 6 = f(3)
 f(x)  is continuous at  x = 3 . It is noted that there is no break in the graph.

Figure 12.5

--- PAGE 233 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
233 Mathematics 11

(b)  g(x) = (x² - 9)/(x - 3)  if  x ≠ 3

As  g(x)  is not defined at  x = 3
 ⇒   g(x)  is discontinuous at  x = 3

It is noted that there is a break in the graph at  x = 3  near  x = 3  as shown in the Figure 12.6.

Example 13 Discuss continuity of  f(x)  at  x = 3 , when
 f(x) =  x - 1   if x < 3 ;
 2x + 1   if x ≥ 3

Solution A sketch of the graph of  f  is shown in the Figure 12.7. We can see that there is a break in the graph at a point when  x = 3 .

Now  f(3) = 2(3) + 1 = 7
 ⇒  Condition (i) is satisfied.

 lim_(x → 3^-) f(x) = lim_(x → 3^-) (x - 1) = 3 - 1 = 2

 lim_(x → 3^+) f(x) = lim_(x → 3^+) (2x + 1) = 6 + 1 = 7

 lim_(x → 3^-) f(x) ≠ lim_(x → 3^+) f(x)

i.e., condition (ii) is not satisfied.

 ∴   lim_(x → 3) f(x)  does not exist.

Hence,  f(x)  is not continuous at  x = 3

12.4 Application of Transcendental Functions to Limits and Continuity on Real World Problems

Limit and continuity of transcendental functions are fundamental concepts in calculus with numerous real-world applications. These concepts help us model, analyze and solve problems in various fields such as growth and decay, finance, economics, surveying and predicting long-term stock prices.

Example 14 Growth and Decay (Radioactive Decay)

The radioactive decay of a substance is given by the function  A(t) = A₀ e^(-kt) , where  A₀  is the initial amount of the substance,  k  is the decay constant, and  t  is the time in years. Find the limit of the amount of substance as  t → ∞ .

Solution

We need to compute the limit: Lim  A(t) = lim_(t → ∞) A₀ e^(-kt)

As  t → ∞ ,  e^(-kt) → 0 , so Lim_(t → ∞) A₀ e^(-kt) = A₀ × 0 = 0

Thus, the amount of radioactive substance approaches 0 as time increases indefinitely.

Example 15 Finance (Compound Interest)

The value of an investment grows according to the formula for continuous compounding  A(t) = P₀ e^(rt) , where  P₀  is the initial principal,  r  is the annual interest rate, and  t  is the time in years. What happens to the value of the investment as  t → ∞ ?

--- PAGE 235 ---

Unit 12 Limit of Sequences and Continuity
Mathematics 11
235

Solution We need to compute the limit: Lim_t→∞ A(t)=Lim P₀e^(r)
Since e^(r) →∞ as t→∞ for any positive r, the value of the investment grows without bound:
Lim_t→∞ P₀e^(r)=∞
Thus, the value of the investment increases indefinitely as time approaches infinity.
Example16 Economics (Supply and Demand)
In economics, the demand function D(p) decreases as the price p increases. Suppose the demand function is given by D(p)=100/(p+1), where p is the price in dollars. Find the limit of the demand as the price becomes very large, i.e., Lim_p→∞ D(p).
Solution Lim_p→∞ D(p)=Lim_p→∞ 100/(p+1)
As p→∞, the denominator becomes very large, so Lim_p→∞ 100/(p+1)=0
Thus, as the price becomes very large, the demand approaches 0.
Example17 Astronomy
The apparent brightness B(d) of a star decreases as the distance from Earth increases following the inverse square law B(d)=L/d², where L is the star's luminosity. Find the limit of the brightness as d→∞.
Solution Lim_d→∞ B(d)=Lim_d→∞ L/d²
As d→∞ the denominator becomes very large, so:
Lim_d→∞ L/d²=0
Thus, as the distance increases indefinitely, the apparent brightness of the star approaches 0.

Unit 13

INTRODUCTION
The ancient Greeks knew the concepts of area, volume, centroids etc., which are related to integral calculus. Later on, in the seventeenth century, Sir Isaac Newton, an English mathematician (1642 – 1727) and Gottfried Wilhelm G. W. Leibniz, a German mathematician, (1646 – 1716) considered the problem of instantaneous rates of change. They reached independently to the invention of differential calculus. After the development of calculus, mathematics became a powerful tool for dealing with rates of change and describing the physical universe.

13.1 Tangent to a Curve at a Point
Let  P(x, f(x))  and  Q(x + δ x, f(x + δ x))  be two points on arc  AB  of graph of  f  defined by the equation  y = f(x)  as shown in Figure 13.1.
Where  δ x  is the increment in the value of  x  (read as delta  x ).

The line  PQ  is secant of the curve and slope of secant line passing through  P(x, f(x))  and  Q(x + δ x, f(x + δ x))  is:
 m_(sec) = RQ/PR = (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x)    (1)

Where  m_(sec)  is slope of the secant line.
Revolving the secant line  PQ  towards  P , some of its successive positions  PQ₁, PQ₂, PQ₃, ...  are shown in the Figure 13.2. Points  Qᵢ (i=1, 2, 3, ...)  are getting closer and closer to the point  P  and  PR  are approaching zero.
In other words, as  δ x → 0 , the point  Q  approaches  P , and the secant line becomes the tangent line. The revolving secant line becomes the tangent line  PT  at  P  while  δ x

Figure 13.1
Figure 13.2

--- PAGE 238 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11
238

approaches zero, that is,

 mₜₐₙ = lim_(δ x → 0) (f(x+δ x)-f(x))/(δ x)    (2)

where  mₜₐₙ  denotes the slope of tangent line. We see that  mₜₐₙ  is the limit of  m_(sec)  as  Q  approaches  P  along the curve  y=f(x) .

Example 1 Find the gradient and an equation of tangent line to the graph of  f(x)=x²-2  at the point  P(-1,-1) .

Solution To find the gradient or slope of the tangent line at point (-1,-1), put  x=-1  in equation (2)

 mₜₐₙ = lim_(δ x → 0) (f(-1+δ x)-f(-1))/(δ x)

 = lim_(δ x → 0) (-1+δ x)²-2-((-1)²-2)/(δ x)

 = lim_(δ x → 0) (1-2δ x+δ x²-2-(1-2))/(δ x)

 = lim_(δ x → 0) (1-2δ x+δ x²-2+1)/(δ x) = lim_(δ x → 0) (-2δ x+δ x²)/(δ x)

 = lim_(δ x → 0) (δ x(-2+δ x))/(δ x) = lim_(δ x → 0) (-2+δ x) = -2

Now to find the equation of tangent line we use the point slope form of equation of line with slope  = -2  and point (-1,-1)

 y-(-1) = -2(x-(-1)) ⇒ y+1 = -2x-2

or  y = -2x-3 , which is the required equation of tangent line.

The graph of  f  and tangent line are shown in the above figure.

13.2 Derivative as the Limit of a Difference Quotient

Let  f  be a real valued function continuous in the interval  (x, xᵢ) ⊆ D_(f)  (domain of  f ), then difference quotient  (f(xᵢ)-f(x))/(xᵢ-x)

represents the average rate of change in the value of  f  with respect to the change  xᵢ-x  in the value of independent variable  x .

--- PAGE 239 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11
239

If  xᵢ  approaches to  x , then lim_(xᵢ → x) (f(xᵢ) - f(x))/(xᵢ - x)

provided this limit exists, is called the instantaneous rate of change of  f  with respect to  x  and is written as  f'(x) .

If  xᵢ = x + δ x  i.e.,  xᵢ - x = δ x , then the expression (i) can be expressed as

(f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x)

and lim_(δ x → 0) (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x)

provided the limit exist, is defined to be the derivative of  f  (or differential coefficient of  f ) with respect to  x  and is denoted by  f'(x)  (read as "f-prime of x"). The domain of  f'  consists of all  x  for which the limit exists. If  x ∈ D_(f)  and  f'(x)  exists, then  f  is said to be differentiable at  x . The process of finding  f'  is called differentiation.

### 13.2.1 Derivative as the Rate of Change of Velocity

The rate of change is a fundamental concept in describing the motion of an object moving in a straight line. In physics, this is typically analyzed using position, velocity, and acceleration, which are all related through derivatives (rates of change).

The position versus time graph provides a simple interpretation of the average velocity over a given time interval.

Suppose a particle moves in a straight line and its position at time  t  is given by the function  s(t) . The average velocity over the interval from  t  to  t₁  denoted by  v_(avg)  is defined as:

 v_(avg) = (s(t₁) - s(t))/(t₁ - t)

Equation (i) also represents the slope of secant line passing through the points  (t, s(t))  and  (t₁, s(t₁)) . If the interval  t₁ - t  is not small, this average velocity does not accurately represent the rate of change at time  t .

To illustrate this, consider a particle whose position at time  t  (in seconds) is given by a function  s(t) = t² + t  in metres. The average rate of change over various time intervals

--- PAGE 240 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11

starting at t = 3 seconds is shown in the table below:

| Interval | t=3 secs to t=5 secs | t=3 secs to t=4 secs | t=3 secs to t=3.5 secs |
|----------|----------------------|----------------------|-------------------------|
| Average velocity | s(5)−s(3)=30−12=9 | s(4)−s(3)=20−12=8 | s(3.5)−s(3)=634−12=7.5 |

<image>
<image>
<image>

We observe that these values are not closely approximate the particle's velocity at exactly 3 seconds. To obtain a better approximation of velocity at x=3, we use smaller intervals:

| Interval | Average velocity |
|----------|------------------|
| t=3 secs to t=3.1 secs | (3.1)²+3.1−12=0.71=7.1 |
| t=3 secs to t=3.01 secs | (3.01)²+3.01−12=0.0701=7.01 |
| t=3 secs to t=3.001 secs | (3.001)²+3.001−12=0.007001=7.001 |

We see as the length of the time interval decreases, the average velocity becomes instantaneous velocity at t=3. Based on the trend, we estimate the instantaneous velocity to be approximately 7 m/sec.

Thus, over a sufficiently small interval, the velocity changes negligibly. If t₁ is very close to t, the average velocity over t₁−t approximates the instantaneous velocity at t. As t₁ approaches t, the average velocity is called the instantaneous velocity.

This is similar to approximating the slope of a tangent line by calculating the slope of a secant line. Mathematically, the instantaneous velocity denoted by vᵢnstantaneous is given by the following limit:

vᵢnstantaneous = Limₜ→t s(t₁)−s(t) / t₁−t (Provide the limit exist)

For convenient, if t₁=t+δt, then as t₁→t ⇒ δt→0, thus above equation becomes:

vᵢnstantaneous = Lim_δt→0 s(t+δt)−s(t) / δt ---- (ii)

--- PAGE 241 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11

In other words, the instantaneous velocity is the derivative of the position function  s(t)  with respect to time.

Example 2 A particle moves along a line such that its position after  t  hours is given by:  s(t) = 4t² + 2t + 1  (in miles)

(a) Find the average velocity over the interval [2, 5]

(b) Find the instantaneous velocity at  t = 3

Solution (a) given position function  s(t) = 4t² + 2t + 1 , where  2 ≤ t ≤ 5

The average velocity over the interval  2 ≤ t ≤ 5  is:

Average velocity  v_(avg) = (s(5) - s(2))/(5 - 2) = (4(5)² + 2(5) + 1 - [4(2)² + 2(2) + 1])/3

 = (111 - 21)/3 = 90/3 = 30  miles/hour

(b) Instantaneous velocity can be found using the formula

Instantaneous velocity  = lim_(δ t → 0) (s(t + δ t) - s(t))/(δ t)

 = lim_(δ t → 0) (4(3 + δ t)² + 2(3 + δ t) + 1 - [4(3)² + 2(3) + 1])/(δ t)

 = lim_(δ t → 0) (4(9 + 6δ t + δ t²) + 6 + 2δ t + 1 - 43)/(δ t)

 = lim_(δ t → 0) (36 + 24δ t + 4δ t² + 6 + 2δ t + 1 - 43)/(δ t)

 = lim_(δ t → 0) (43 + 26δ t + 4δ t² - 43)/(δ t) = lim_(δ t → 0) (26δ t + 4δ t²)/(δ t)

 = lim_(δ t → 0) (δ t(26 + 4δ t))/(δ t) = lim_(δ t → 0) (26 + 4δ t) = 26

Thus, instantaneous velocity at  t = 3  is 26 miles/hour

13.3 Process of Finding Derivative  f'(x)  by Definition

12.3.1 Notation of Derivative

Several notations are used for derivatives. We have used the functional symbol  f'(x) , for the derivative of  f  at  x . For the function  y = f(x) .

 y + δ y = f(x + δ x)

--- PAGE 242 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11

Dividing both the sides of (iv) by δx, we get
δy/δx = f(x + δx) - f(x) / δx
(v)

Taking limit of both the sides of (v) as δx → 0, we have
Lim δy/δx = Lim f(x + δx) - f(x) / δx
(vi)

Lim δy/δx is denoted by dy/dx, so (vi) is written as dy/dx = f'(x)

Note The symbol dy/dx is used for the derivative of y with respect to x and here it is not a quotient of dy and dx. dy/dx is also denoted by y'.

Now we write, in a table the notations for derivative of y = f(x) used by different mathematicians:

| Name of mathematician | Leibniz | Newton | Lagrange | Euler |
|------------------------|---------|---------|----------|-------|
| Notation used for derivative | dy/dx or df/dx | f'(x) or y' | f'(x) | Df(x) |

If we replace x + δx by x and x by a, then the expression f(x + δx) - f(x) becomes f(x) - f(a) and the change δx in the independent variable, in this case, is x - a.

So, the expression f(x + δx) - f(x) is written as f(x) - f(a) / x - a
(vii)

Taking the limit of the expression (vii) when x → a, gives Lim f(x) - f(a) / x - a = f'(a).

Here f'(a) is called the derivative or gradient of f at x = a.

13.3.2 Finding f'(x) by Definition of Derivative

Given a function f, then f'(x) if it exists, can be found by the following four steps:
Step I: Find f(x + δx)
Step II: Simplify f(x + δx) - f(x)
Step III: Divide f(x + δx) - f(x) by δx to get f(x + δx) - f(x) / δx and simplify it.
Step IV: Find Lim f(x + δx) - f(x) / δx

The method of finding derivatives by this process is called differentiation by definition or by ab-initio or from first principle.

--- PAGE 243 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11
Page 243

Example 3 Find the derivative of the following functions by definition
(a)  f(x) = c
(b)  f(x) = x²

Solution
(a)  f(x) = c
(i)  f(x + δ x) = c
(ii)  f(x + δ x) - f(x) = c - c = 0
(iii)  (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) = 0/(δ x) = 0
(iv)  lim_(δ x → 0) (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) = lim_(δ x → 0) (0) = 0

Thus,  f'(x) = 0 , that is,  d/dx(c) = 0

(b)  f(x) = x²
(i)  f(x + δ x) = (x + δ x)²
(ii)  f(x + δ x) - f(x) = (x + δ x)² - x² = x² + 2xδ x + (δ x)² - x² = (2x + δ x)δ x
(iii)  (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) = ((2x + δ x)δ x)/(δ x) = 2x + δ x ,  (δ x ≠ 0)
(iv)  lim_(δ x → 0) (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) = lim_(δ x → 0) (2x + δ x) = 2x

i.e.,  f'(x) = 2x

Example 4 Find the derivative of  √x  at  x = a  from first principle.

Solution If  f(x) = √x , then
(i)  f(x + δ x) = √(x + δ x)  and
(ii)  f(x + δ x) - f(x) = √(x + δ x) - √x
 = (√(x + δ x) - √x)(√(x + δ x) + √x)/(√(x + δ x) + √x)  (rationalizing the numerator)
 = (x + δ x - x)/(√(x + δ x) + √x)
i.e.,  f(x + δ x) - f(x) = (δ x)/(√(x + δ x) + √x)

(iii) Dividing both sides of (1) by  δ x , we have
 (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) = (δ x)/(δ x(√(x + δ x) + √x))
 = 1/(√(x + δ x) + √x) ,  (δ x ≠ 0)

--- PAGE 244 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation 244 Mathematics 11

(iv) Taking limit of both the sides as δx → 0, we have

Lim δx→0 f(x+δx)-f(x)/ δx = Lim δx→0(1/ √x+δx+√x )

i.e., f'(x)=1 /√x+√x =1 /2√x , (x>0) and f'(a)=1/ 2√a

Alternate method: Putting x=a in f(x)=√x, gives f(a)=√a

So, f(x)-f(a)=√x-√a

Using alternative form for the definition of the derivative, we have

f(x)-f(a) /x-a =√x-√a / x-a

=(√x-√a)(√x+√a) /(x-a)(√x+√a) (rationalizing the numerator)

= x-a/ (x-a)(√x+√a) =1 /√x+√a , (x≠a) (2)

Taking limit of both the sides of (2) as x→a, gives

Lim x→a f(x)-f(a)/ x-a = Lim x→a 1/ √x+√a =1/ √a+√a

i.e., f'(a)=1 /2√a

which is the gradient of f at x=a.

13.4 Derivation of xⁿ where n ∈ Z

(a) We find the derivative of xⁿ when n is positive integer.
(b) Let y = xⁿ. Then
y + δy = (x + δx)ⁿ
and δy = (x + δx)ⁿ − xⁿ

Using the binomial theorem, we have

δy = xⁿ + nxⁿ⁻¹ ⋅ δx + ((n(n−1)xⁿ⁻²)(δx)²)/2! + ... + (δx)ⁿ

i.e., δy = δx [nxⁿ⁻¹ + (n(n−1)xⁿ⁻²)/2! ⋅ δx + ... + (δx)ⁿ⁻¹ 

Dividing both sides of (i) by δx, gives

δy = nxⁿ⁻¹ + n(n−1)xⁿ⁻² ⋅ δx + ... + (δx)ⁿ⁻¹
δx

Note that each term on the right hand side of (ii) involves δx except the first term, so taking the limit as δx → 0, we get dy = nxⁿ⁻¹
dx

As y = xⁿ, so d (xⁿ) = n ⋅ xⁿ⁻¹
dx

--- PAGE 246 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11
Page 246

(b) Let  y = xⁿ  where  n  is negative integer.
Let  n = -m  ( m  is a positive integer). Then

 y = x^(-m) = 1/(x^(m))  ... (i)

and  y + δ y = 1/((x + δ x)^(m))  ... (ii)

Subtracting (i) from (ii), gives

 δ y = 1/((x + δ x)^(m)) - 1/(x^(m)) = (x^(m) - (x + δ x)^(m))/(x^(m) (x + δ x)^(m))

 = (x^(m) - [x^(m) + mx^(m-1) δ x + (m(m-1))/(2!) x^(m-2) (δ x)² + … + (δ x)^(m)])/(x^(m) (x + δ x)^(m))

(expanding  (x + δ x)^(m)  by binomial theorem)

 = - δ x ( (mx^(m-1) + (m(m-1))/(2!) x^(m-2) δ x + … + (δ x)^(m-1))/(x^(m) (x + δ x)^(m)) )

and  (δ y)/(δ x) = -1/(x^(m) (x + δ x)^(m)) · ( mx^(m-1) + (m(m-1))/(2!) x^(m-2) δ x + … + (δ x)^(m-1) )

Taking limit when  δ x → 0 , we get

 dy/dx = -1/(x^(m) · x^(m)) · mx^(m-1)  (all terms containing  δ x  vanish)

 = -mx^(m-1) · x^(m-1)

 dx = nxⁿ⁻¹

or  (d(xⁿ))/dx = nxⁿ⁻¹

So, we have proved that  d/dx(xⁿ) = nxⁿ⁻¹ , if  n ∈ Z

The above rule also holds if  n ∈ Q-Z , i.e., for rational powers.

For example,  d/dx(x^(2/3)) = 2/3 x^(2/3-1) = 2/(3x^(1/3))

The proof of  d/dx(xⁿ) = nxⁿ⁻¹  when  n ∈ Q-Z  is left as an exercise.

Note
 d/dx(xⁿ) = nxⁿ⁻¹  is called power rule. Where  n ∈ R

13.5 Connection Between Derivatives and Continuity

Calculus is a powerful branch of mathematics that allows us to study change and motion. Two of its foundational concepts of continuity and derivatives are deeply connected. While each concept has its own definition and application, understanding how they relate to each other is essential for solving real-world problems in mathematics.

As discussed in previous units, a function is continuous at a point if its graph has no breaks, jumps, or holes at that point. On the other hand, the derivative of a function at a point measures the instantaneous rate of change or equivalently, the slope of the tangent line at that point. However, this definition depends on the function being well-behaved around the point. This leads to a well-known result:

If a function is differentiable at a point, it must also be continuous there. This means that differentiability implies continuity, but the reverse is not necessarily true. For example, consider the function  f(x) = |x| , clearly this function is continuous at  x = 0  (see Figure 13.3). Now we check the differentiability of  f(x) = |x|  at  x = 0 .

 f(x) = |x|
 f(0) = |0| = 0
so
 f(0 + δ x) - f(0) = |δ x| - 0
and
 (f(0 + δ x) - f(0))/(δ x) = (|δ x|)/(δ x)

Thus
 f'(x) = lim_(δ x → 0) (|δ x|)/(δ x)

Because  |δ x| = δ x  when  δ x > 0
and  |δ x| = -δ x  when  δ x < 0 ,
so, we consider one-sided limits

 lim_(δ x → 0^+) (|δ x|)/(δ x) = lim_(δ x → 0^+) (δ x)/(δ x) = 1
and
 lim_(δ x → 0^-) (|δ x|)/(δ x) = lim_(δ x → 0^-) (-δ x)/(δ x) = -1

The right-hand and left-hand limits are not equal, therefore, the  lim_(δ x → 0) (|δ x|)/(δ x)  does not exist.

This implies that the derivative of  f  at  x = 0  does not exist, and thus, there is no tangent line to the graph of  f  at this point (see Figure 13.3). However, the derivative exists at all other points of  f , i.e., it is 1 on the right side and -1 on the left side. A function can be continuous at a point but not necessarily differentiable there.

Mathematics 11

(i) Find the average velocity over the interval [1, 4]
(ii) Find the instantaneous velocity at t=2

11. A stone is thrown upwards and its height after t seconds is given by:
s(t)=-16t²+32t+10 (in feet). Find the instantaneous velocity at t=1

12. The outdoor temperature (in °C) over time is modeled by: T(t)=-t²+12t+10, where t is the time in hours. Find the instantaneous rate of change at t=2.

13.6 Theorems on Differentiation

We have, so far, proved the following two formulas:
1. d/dx(c)=0 that is, the derivative of a constant function is zero.
2. d/dx(xⁿ)=nxⁿ⁻¹, power formula (or rule) when n is any real number.

--- PAGE 249 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
249 Mathematics 11

Now we will prove other important formulas (or rules) which are used to determine derivatives of different functions efficiently. Henceforth, in all subsequent discussion, f, g, h etc., all denote functions differentiable at x, unless stated otherwise.

3. Derivative of  y = cf(x)

Proof: Let  y = cf(x) , Then
(i)  y + δ y = cf(x + δ x)  and
(ii)  y + δ y - y = cf(x + δ x) - cf(x)
or  δ y = c[f(x + δ x) - f(x)]  (Factoring out c)
(iii)  (δ y)/(δ x) = c [ (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) ]
Taking limit when  δ x → 0
(iv)  lim_(δ x → 0) (δ y)/(δ x) = lim_(δ x → 0) c · (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) = c lim_(δ x → 0) (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x)
A constant factor can be taken out from a limit sign.
Thus,  dy/dx = cf'(x) , that is  [cf(x)]' = cf'(x)  or  d/dx[cf(x)] = c d/dx[f(x)]

Example 6 Calculate  d/dx(3x⁴) = 3 d/dx(x³)  (Using Formula 3)
Solution  = 3 × 4/3 x³⁻¹ = 4x³  (Using power rule)

4. Derivative of a sum or a difference of functions
If  f  and  g  are differentiable at  x , then  f + g ,  f - g  are also differentiable at  x  and
 [f(x) + g(x)]' = f'(x) + g'(x) , that is  d/dx[f(x) + g(x)] = d/dx[f(x)] + d/dx[g(x)]
Also  [f(x) - g(x)]' = f'(x) - g'(x) , that is  d/dx[f(x) - g(x)] = d/dx[f(x)] - d/dx[g(x)]

Proof: Let  φ(x) = f(x) + g(x) . Then
(i)  φ(x + δ x) = f(x + δ x) + g(x + δ x)  and
(ii)  φ(x + δ x) - φ(x) = f(x + δ x) + g(x + δ x) - [f(x) + g(x)]
 = [f(x + δ x) - f(x)] + [g(x + δ x) - g(x)]  (rearranging the terms)
(iii)  (φ(x + δ x) - φ(x))/(δ x) = (f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) + (g(x + δ x) - g(x))/(δ x)
Taking the limit when  δ x → 0

--- PAGE 250 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation

( iv) lim _(Δ x → 0) (φ(x+Δ x)-φ(x))/(Δ x)=lim _(Δ x → 0)[(f(x+Δ x)-f(x))/(Δ x)+(g(x+Δ x)-g(x))/(Δ x)]

=lim _(Δ x → 0) (f(x+Δ x)-f(x))/(Δ x)+lim _(Δ x → 0) (g(x+Δ x)-g(x))/(Δ x)

(The limit of a sum is the sum of the limits)

φ'(x)=f'(x)+g'(x), that is [f(x)+g(x)]'=f'(x)+g'(x)

or d/dx[f(x)+g(x)]=d/dx[f(x)]+d/dx[g(x)]

The proof for the second part is similar.

Note Sum or difference formula can be extended to find derivative of more than two functions.

Example 7 Find the derivative of y=3/4x⁴+2/3x³+1/2x²+2x+5 w.r.t. x.

Solution y=3/4x⁴+2/3x³+1/2x²+2x+5

Differentiating with respect to x, we have

dy/dx=d/dx[3/4x⁴+2/3x³+1/2x²+2x+5]=d/dx(3/4x⁴)+d/dx(2/3x³)+d/dx(1/2x²)+d/dx(2x)+d/dx(5)(5)

(Using formula 4)

=3/4d(x⁴)+2/3d(x³)+1/2d(x²)+2d(x)+0 (Using formula 3 and 1)

=3/4(4x⁴⁻¹)+2/3(3x³⁻¹)+1/2(2x²⁻¹)+2(1.x¹⁻¹) (By power formula)

=3x³+2x²+x+2

=5x⁴+15x²+14x

Example 9 Find the derivative of  y = (2√x + 2)(x - √x)

Solution  y = (2√x + 2)(x - √x)

 = 2(√x + 1)·√x(√x - 1) = 2√x(√x + 1)(√x - 1)

 = 2√x(x - 1) = 2(x^(3/2) - x^(1/2))

Differentiating with respect to  x  we have

dy/dx = d/dx[2(x^(3/2) - x^(1/2))]

 = 2[d/dx x^(3/2) - d/dx x^(1/2)] = 2[3/2x^(3/2-1) - 1/2x^(1/2-1)]

 = 3x^(1/2) - x^(-1/2) = 3√x - 1/(√x) = (3x - 1)/(√x)

5. Derivative of a Product (The Product Rule)

If  f  and  g  are differentiable at  x , then  fg  is also differentiable at  x  and

[f(x)g(x)]' = f'(x)g(x) + f(x)g'(x), that is

d/dx[f(x)g(x)] = [d/dx[f(x)]]g(x) + f(x)[d/dx[g(x)]]

Proof: Let  φ(x) = f(x)g(x) . Then

(i)  φ(x + δ x) = f(x + δ x)g(x + δ x)  and

(ii)  φ(x + δ x) - φ(x) = f(x + δ x)g(x + δ x) - f(x)g(x)

Subtracting and adding  f(x)g(x + δ x)  in step (ii), gives

 φ(x + δ x) - φ(x) = f(x + δ x)g(x + δ x) - f(x)g(x + δ x) + f(x)g(x + δ x) - f(x)g(x)

 = [f(x + δ x) - f(x)]g(x + δ x) + f(x)[g(x + δ x) - g(x)]

(iii)  (φ(x + δ x) - φ(x))/(δ x) = [(f(x + δ x) - f(x))/(δ x)]g(x + δ x) + f(x)[(g(x + δ x) - g(x))/(δ x)]

Taking limit when  δ x → 0

--- PAGE 252 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11
252

(iv) lim_(δ x → 0) (φ(x+δ x)-φ(x))/(δ x)

= lim_(δ x → 0) [(f(x+δ x)-f(x))/(δ x) · g(x+δ x) + f(x) · (g(x+δ x)-g(x))/(δ x)]

= lim_(δ x → 0) (f(x+δ x)-f(x))/(δ x) · lim_(δ x → 0) g(x+δ x) + lim_(δ x → 0) f(x) · lim_(δ x → 0) (g(x+δ x)-g(x))/(δ x)  using limit thoeorm 

Thus φ'(x) = f'(x) · g(x) + f(x) · g'(x)

[lim_(δ x → 0) g(x+δ x) = g(x)]

or d/dx[f(x) · g(x)] = d/dx[f(x)] · g(x) + f(x) [d/dx g(x)]

Example 10 Find derivative of y = (2√x+2)(x-√x) with respect to x.

Solution y = (2√x+2)(x-√x)

Differentiating with respect to x, we get

dy/dx = 2 d/dx[(√x+1)(x-√x)]

= 2 [(d/dx(√x+1))(x-√x)+(√x+1) d/dx(x-√x)]

= 2 [(1/2x^(1/2-1)+0)(x-√x)+(√x+1) × (1-1/2x^(1/2-1))]

= 2 [1/(2√x)(x-√x)+(√x+1) × (1-1/(2√x))]

= 2 [(x-√x)/(2√x)+(√x+1) ((2√x-1)/(2√x))]

= 1/(√x)[x-√x+2x-√x+2√x-1]

= (3x-1)/(√x)

6. Derivative of a Quotient (The Quotient Rule)

If  f  and  g  are differentiable at  x  and  g(x) ≠ 0 , for any  x ∈ D(g)  then  f/g  is differentiable at  x  and

((f(x))/(g(x)))' = (f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x))/([g(x)]²)

that is

d/dx[(f(x))/(g(x))] = ([d/dx[f(x)]g(x) - f(x)[d/dx[g(x)]]])/([g(x)]²)


Proof:
Let  φ(x) = (f(x))/(g(x)) . Then
(i)  φ(x+δ x) = (f(x+δ x))/(g(x+δ x))  and
(ii)  φ(x+δ x) - φ(x) = (f(x+δ x))/(g(x+δ x)) - (f(x))/(g(x)) = (f(x+δ x)g(x) - f(x)g(x+δ x))/(g(x)g(x+δ x))

Subtracting and adding  f(x)g(x)  in the numerator of step (ii), gives

φ(x+δ x) - φ(x) = (f(x+δ x)g(x) - f(x)g(x+δ x) + f(x)g(x))/(g(x)g(x+δ x))


= 1/(g(x)g(x+δ x))[(f(x+δ x) - f(x))g(x) - f(x)(g(x+δ x) - g(x))]


(iii)  (φ(x+δ x) - φ(x))/(δ x) = 1/(g(x)g(x+δ x))[(f(x+δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) · g(x) - f(x) · (g(x+δ x) - g(x))/(δ x)]

Taking limit when  δ x → 0
(iv)  lim_(δ x → 0) (φ(x+δ x) - φ(x))/(δ x)

= lim_(δ x → 0) 1/(g(x)g(x+δ x))[(f(x+δ x) - f(x))/(δ x) · g(x) - f(x) · (g(x+δ x) - g(x))/(δ x)]


Using limit theorems, we have

φ'(x) = 1/(g(x) · g(x))[f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)]


∴ lim_(δ x → 0) g(x+δ x) = g(x)

--- PAGE 254 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11

Thus ((f(x))/(g(x)))' = (f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x))/([g(x)]²)

or d/dx((f(x))/(g(x))) = [d/dx(f(x))]g(x) - f(x)[d/dx(g(x))]

d/dx((2x³ - 3x² + 5)/(x² + 1))

Example 11 Differentiate (2x³ - 3x² + 5)/(x² + 1) with respect to x.

Solution Let φ(x) = (2x³ - 3x² + 5)/(x² + 1). Then

f(x) = 2x³ - 3x² + 5 and g(x) = x² + 1

Now f'(x) = d/dx[2x³ - 3x² + 5] = 2(3x²) - 3(2x) + 0 = 6x² - 6x

and g'(x) = d/dx[x² + 1] = 2x + 0 = 2x

Using the quotient formula φ'(x) = (f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x))/([g(x)]²), We obtain

d/dx[(2x³ - 3x² + 5)/(x² + 1)] = (6x² - 6x)(x² + 1) - (2x³ - 3x² + 5)(2x)/((x² + 1)²)

= (6x⁴ - 6x³ + 6x² - 6x - (4x⁴ - 6x³ + 10x))/((x² + 1)²)

= (6x⁴ - 6x³ + 6x² - 6x - 4x⁴ + 6x³ - 10x)/((x² + 1)²)

= (2x⁴ + 6x² - 16x)/((x² + 1)²)

13.7 Application of Differentiation

We will apply the concept of differentiation to real-world problems such as profits on diminishing returns, environmental factors, financial investments, population growth, spread of diseases, movement of particles, time-speed in transportation, structural stress, material required that changes in construction.

Profits on Diminishing Returns

Example 12 A company's profit function is given by P(x) = 100x - 5x², where x is the number of units produced. Determine the marginal profit when x = 8 units.

Solution The marginal profit is the derivative of the profit function with respect to x.
P'(x) = d/dx(100x - 5x²) = 100 - 10x

Now, substitute x = 8: P'(8) = 100 - 10(8) = 20

So, the marginal profit is 20 when 8 units are produced (in the given currency).

Movement of Particles

Example 13 A particle moves along a line according to the position function s(t) = 4t³ - 3t² + 2t, where s(t) is the position in metres and t is the time in seconds. Find the velocity and acceleration at t = 2 seconds.

Solution Velocity is the derivative of the position function:
v(t) = d/dt(4t³ - 3t² + 2t) = 12t² - 6t + 2

--- PAGE 256 ---

Unit 13 Differentiation
Mathematics 11
256

Substitute t = 2:
v(2) = 12(2)² - 6(2) + 2 = 48 - 12 + 2 = 38
So, the velocity at t = 2 is 38 m/s.

Acceleration is the derivative of the velocity function:
a(t) = d/dt (12t² - 6t + 2) = 24t - 6

Substitute t = 2:
a(2) = 24(2) - 6 = 48 - 6 = 42
So, the acceleration at t = 2 is 42 m/s².

Financial Investments
Example 14 A bank offers a compound interest rate on an investment, and the value of the investment after t years is given by V(t) = 5000(1+0.04t)². Find the rate of change of the investment value after 10 years.

Solution The rate of change of the investment is the derivative of V(t) with respect to t.
V'(t) = d/dt (5000(1+0.04t)²) = 5000(2)(1+0.04t)(0.04)
V'(t) = 400(1+0.04t)

Substitute t = 10:
V'(10) = 400(1+0.04×10) = 400(1+0.40) = 400×1.4 = 560
So, the investment is growing at a rate of Rs.560 per year after 10 years.

Structural Stress
Example 15 The stress on a beam under a varying load is modeled by S(x) = 500x - 2x³, where S(x) is the stress in pascals (Pa) and x is the distance (in metres) from the beam's fixed end. Find the rate of change of stress at x = 5 metres.

Solution The rate of change of stress is the derivative of S(x) with respect to x.
S'(x) = d/dx (500x - 2x³) = 500 - 6x²

Substitute x = 5:
S'(5) = 500 - 6(5)² = 500 - 6×25 = 500 - 150 = 350
So, the stress is increasing at a rate of 350 Pa per metre at x = 5 metres.

Unit 14
Vectors in Space

INTRODUCTION
In this unit, we will look into the rectangular coordinate system in three-dimensional space and explore the fundamental mathematical operations involving vectors in space. We will begin by understanding the dot product (or scalar product) and the cross product (or vector product) of two vectors and learn about their geometric interpretation. Further, we emphasize their practical applications. For example, we will see how these concepts can be used to calculate the area of a triangle and the area of a parallelogram. Finally, we will explore the extensive use of vectors in three-dimensional space, particularly in physics, where they play an important role in determining forces, velocities, and other essential physical quantities. For example, determining the work done by a constant force when moving an object along a specified vector.

14.1 Vectors (Recall)
In previous classes, we learned about two fundamental quantities: scalars and vectors. A scalar is a quantity that has only magnitude or size, such as mass, time, density, temperature, length, volume, speed, work etc. On the other hand, a vector is a quantity that has both magnitude and direction, for example displacement, velocity, acceleration, weight, force, momentum, electric and magnetic fields, etc. Geometrically, a vector is represented as a directed line segment ⃗AB with A as its initial point and B as the terminal point.

In two-dimension (R²) a vector has components that can be represented by an ordered pair [x, y] of real numbers. For the vector u = [x, y], x and y represent the components of u. Addition of Vectors: For any two vectors u = [x₁, y₁] and v = [x₂, y₂], we have
u + v = [x₁, y₁] + [x₂, y₂] = [x₁ + x₂, y₁ + y₂]

Scalar Multiplication of a Vector: For u = [x, y] and a ∈ R, we have
au = a[x, y] = [ax, ay]

Equal Vectors: Two vectors u = [x₁, y₁] and v = [x₂, y₂] of R² are said to be equal

--- PAGE 259 ---

Unit 14 Vectors in Space
Mathematics 11
Page 259

if and only if they have the same components. That is, [x₁, y₁] = [x₂, y₂] if and only if x₁ = x₂ and y₁ = y₂ and we write u⃗ = v⃗.

In other words, two vectors u⃗ and v⃗ are said to be equal, if they have same magnitude and same direction.

Parallel Vectors: Two vectors are parallel if and only if they are non-zero scalar multiples of each other.

For example, vectors ⃗AB, -⃗AB and 3/2⃗AB are parallel.

Magnitude of a Vector

The magnitude (or norm or length) of a vector in 2D represents the length of the vector from the origin to the point represented by the vector. For any vector u⃗ = [x, y] in R², we define the magnitude, as the distance of the point P(x, y) from the origin O.

Magnitude of ⃗OP = |⃗OP| = |u⃗| = √(x² + y²)

Now, we will learn some mathematical operations involving vectors in three-dimensional space.

14.1.1 Rectangular Coordinate System in Space

In space a rectangular coordinate system is constructed using three mutually orthogonal (perpendicular) axes, which have origin as their common point of intersection. When sketching figures, we follow the convention that the positive x-axis points towards the reader, the positive y-axis to the right and the positive z-axis points upwards.

These axes are also labeled in accordance with the right-hand rule. The fingers of the right hand, pointing in the direction of the positive x-axis, curled images toward the positive y-axis, and the thumb will point in the direction of the positive z-axis.

A point P in space has three coordinates, one along x-axis, the second along y-axis and the third along z-axis. If the

14.1.2 Concept of a Vector in Space
The set  R³ = (x, y, z): x, y, z ∈ R  is called 3-dimensional space. An element (x, y, z) of  R³  represents a point  P(x, y, z) , which is uniquely determined by its coordinates  x ,  y  and  z . Given a vector  underlineu  in space, there exists a unique point  P(x, y, z)  in space such that the vector ⃗OP  is equal to  underlineu  (see figure). Now each element (x, y, z) ∈ R³ is associated with a unique ordered triple (x, y, z), which represents the vector  underline_u = ⃗OP = [x, y, z] .

14.1.3 Fundamental Mathematical Operations for Vectors in Space
We define addition and scalar multiplication in  R³  by:

(i) Addition of Vectors: For any two vectors  underline_u = [x, y, z]  and  underline_v = [x', y', z']  we have
 underline_u + underline_v = [x, y, z] + [x', y', z'] = [x + x', y + y', z + z']

(ii) Scalar Multiplication of a Vector: For  underline_u = [x, y, z]  and  a ∈ R , we have
 a underline_u = a[x, y, z] = [ax, ay, az]

The set of all ordered triples [x, y, z] of real numbers, together with the rules of addition and scalar multiplication is called the set of vectors in  R³ . For the vector  underlineu = [x, y, z] ,  x ,  y  and  z  are called the components of  underline_u . The definition of vectors in  R³  states that vector addition and scalar multiplication are to be carried out also for vectors in space just as for vectors in the plane. Similarly, we define in  R³ :

(a) The negative of the vector  underline_u = [x, y, z]  as  -underline_u = (-1)underline_u = [-x, -y, -z]

(b) The difference of two vectors  underline_v = [x', y', z']  and  underline_w = [x'', y'', z'']  as
 underline_v - underline_w = underline_v + (-underline_w) = [x' - x'', y' - y'', z' - z'']

(c) The zero vector as  underline_O = [0, 0, 0]

(d) Equality of two vectors: Two vectors  underline_v = [x', y', z']  and  underline_w = [x'', y'', z'']  are equal if and only if  x' = x'' ,  y' = y''  and  z' = z'' .

(e) Position Vector
For any point  P(x, y, z)  in  R³ , a vector  underline_u = [x, y, z]  is represented by a directed line segment  OP , whose initial point is at origin. Such vectors are called position vectors in  R³ .

Mathematics 11

we define the magnitude, norm, or length of a vector u =[x,y,z] in space by the distnace of the point P(x,y,z) form the origin O.

14.1.4 Magnitude of a Vector in Space
We define the magnitude, norm, or length of a vector  underlineu = [x, y, z]  in space by the distance of the point  P(x, y, z)  from the origin  O .
∴  |overline_(OP)| = |underline_u| = √(x² + y² + z²)

Example 1 For the vectors,  underline_u = [1, -2, 3] ,  underline_v = [2, 1, 3]  and  underline_w = [-1, 4, 0] , find the following:
(i)  underline_v + underline_w
(ii)  2underline_w
(iii)  |underline_u|
(iv)  |underline_v - 2underline_w|
(v)  |2underline_u - underline_v + 3underline_w|

Solution
(i)  underline_v + underline_w = [2 - 1, 1 + 4, 3 + 0] = [1, 5, 3]
(ii)  2underline_w = 2[-1, 4, 0] = [-2, 8, 0]
(iii)  |underline_u| = ||1, -2, 3|| = √((1)² + (-2)² + (3)²) = √(1 + 4 + 9) = √14
(iv)  |underline_v - 2underline_w| = ||2 + 2, 1 - 8, 3 - 0|| = ||4, -7, 3||
 = √((4)² + (-7)² + (3)²) = √(16 + 49 + 9) = √74
(v)  |2*underline_u - underline_v + 3*underline_w| = ||2[1, -2, 3] - [2, 1, 3] + 3[-1, 4, 0]|| = [[2, -4, 6] - [2, 1, 3] + [-3, 12, 0]]
 = [-3, 7, 3] = √((-3)² + (7)² + (3)²) = √(9 + 49 + 9) = √67

14.1.5 Components of a Vector
As in plane, we introduce three special vectors  i = [1, 0, 0] ,  j = [0, 1, 0]  and  k = [0, 0, 1]  in  ℝ³ .

As magnitude of  i = √(1² + 0² + 0²) = 1
magnitude of  j = √(0² + 1² + 0²) = 1  and
magnitude of  k = √(0² + 0² + 1²) = 1 . So,  i ,  j  and  k  are called unit vectors along x-axis, y-axis and z-axis respectively. Using the definition of addition and scalar multiplication, the vector  [x, y, z]  can be written as:
 underlineu = [x, y, z] = [x, 0, 0] + [0, y, 0] + [0, 0, z]
 = x[1, 0, 0] + y[0, 1, 0] + z[0, 0, 1] = xi + yj + zk

Thus, each vector  [x, y, z]  in  ℝ³  can be uniquely represented by  xi + yj + zk .

Unit Vector
A unit vector is defined as a vector whose magnitude is unity. In three-dimensional space the unit vector of the vector  underline_u = xi + yj + zk  is written as  u^ (read as u hat) and

--- PAGE 262 ---

Unit 14 Vectors in Space
Mathematics 11
Page 262

is defined by
û = (u)/(|u|) = x/(√(x² + y² + z²))* i + y/(√(x² + y² + z²)) *j + z/(√(x² + y² + z²)) *k

In terms of unit vector i, j and k, the sum u + v of two vectors.
u = [x₁, y₁, z₁] and v = [x₂, y₂, z₂] is written as:

u + v = [x₁ + x₂, y₁ + y₂, z₁ + z₂]


= (x₁ + x₂)i + (y₁ + y₂)j + (z₁ + z₂)k


Example 2 Find the unit vector of u = 2i + 5j - k.
Solution Given vector u = 2i + 5j - k, to find the unit vector

⇒ |u| = √((2)² + (5)² + (-1)²) = √30

The unit vector is:

⇒ û = (u)/(|u|) = (2i + 5j - k)/(√30) = 1/(√30)(2i + 5j - k)

Thus, û = 1/(√30)(2i + 5j - k) is the required unit vector.

Example 3 If u = 2i + 3j + k, v = 4i + 6j + 2k and w = -6i - 9j - 3k, then show that u, v and w are parallel to each other.
Solution v = 4i + 6j + 2k = 2(2i + 3j + k)

∴    v = 2u

⇒ u and v are parallel vectors.
w = -6i - 9j - 3k

= -3(2i + 3j + k)    ∴    w = -3u

⇒ u and w are parallel vectors.
Hence u, v and w are parallel to each other.

14.1.6 Properties of Vectors Let u, v and w be vectors in the plane or in space and let a, b ∈ ℝ, then they have the following properties:
(i) u + v = v + u (Commutative property)
(ii) (u + v) + w = u + (v + w) (Associative property)
(iii) u + o = u (Additive Identity)
(iv) u + (-1)u = u - u = o (Inverse for vector addition)
(v) a(v + w) = av + aw (Distributive property)
(vi) a(bu) = (ab)u (Scalar multiplication)

--- PAGE 263 ---

Unit 14 Vectors in Space
Mathematics 11

Proof: (i) Since for any two real numbers a, b ∈ ℝ, a + b = b + a, it follows that for any two vectors underlineu = [x, y, z] and underlinev = [x', y', z'] in R³, where components of underline_u and underline_v belong to ℝ.

We have

underline_u + underline_v = [x, y, z] + [x', y', z']


= [x + x', y + y', z + z']


= [x' + x, y' + y, z' + z]


= [x', y', z'] + [x, y, z]


= underline_v + underline_u


So, addition of vectors in R³ is commutative.

(ii) Since for any three real numbers a, b, c ∈ ℝ, (a + b) + c = a + (b + c), it follows that for any three vectors underlineu = [x, y, z], underlinev = [x', y', z'] and underline_w = [x'', y'', z''] in R³. Where components of underline_u, underline_v and underline_w belong to ℝ.

We have

(underline_u + underline_v) + underline_w = [x + x', y + y', z + z'] + [x'', y'', z'']


= [(x + x') + x'', (y + y') + y'', (z + z') + z'']


= [x + (x' + x''), y + (y' + y''), z + (z' + z'')]


= [x, y, z] + [x' + x'', y' + y'', z' + z'']


= underline_u + (underline_v + underline_w)


So, addition of vectors in R³ is associative.

(iii) Since for any real number a and 0
a + 0 = a, it follows that for any vectors underlineu = [x, y, z], and underlineo = [0, 0, 0], where underline_o is the zero vector in R³.

We have

underline_u + underline_o = [x, y, z] + [0, 0, 0]


= [x + 0, y + 0, z + 0]


= [x, y, z] = underline_u


underline_u + underline_o = underline_u


Thus, underline_o is the additive identity in R³.

(iv) Since for any real number a, there exist -a such that a + (-a) = a - a = 0, it follows that for any vector underlineu = [x, y, z], there exists -underlineu = [-x, -y, -z] in R³.

Such that

underline_u + (-underline_u) = [x, y, z] + [-x, -y, -z] = [x + (-x), y + (-y), z + (-z)]


= [0, 0, 0] = underline_o, where underline_o is the additive identity


underline_u + (-underline_u) = underline_o


Thus -underlineu is the additive inverse of underlineu in R³.

The proofs of the other parts are left as an exercise for the students.

14.1.7 Distance Between Two Points in Space

If ⃗_OP₁ and ⃗_OP₂ are the position vectors of the points P₁(x₁, y₁, z₁) and P₂(x₂, y₂, z₂)

The vector ⃗P₁P₂ is given by

⃗P₁P₂ = ⃗OP₂ - ⃗OP₁ = [x₂ - x₁, y₂ - y₁, z₂ - z₁]


Distance between P₁ and P₂ = |⃗P₁P₂|


= √((x₂ - x₁)² + (y₂ - y₁)² + (z₂ - z₁)²)


This is called distance formula between two points P₁ and P₂ in R³.

Example 4 Suppose a butterfly's flight path passed through points (2, 4, 7) and (6, 1, 3), where each unit represents a metre. What is the magnitude of the displacement the butterfly experienced in traveling between these two points?

Solution Distance between two points in three-dimensional space is given by the formula


d = √((x₂ - x₁)² + (y₂ - y₁)² + (z₂ - z₁)²)


Substitute the coordinates of the given points into the formula:


d = √((6-2)² + (1-4)² + (3-7)²)


d = √(16 + 9 + 16) = √41 = 6.40


The magnitude of the displacement the butterfly experienced in traveling between these two points is approximately 6.40 metres.

14.1.8 Direction Angles and Direction Cosines of a Vector

Let underline_r = ⃗OP = xi + yj + zk be a non-zero vector, let α, β and γ denote the angles formed between underline_r and the unit coordinate vectors i, j and k respectively,

where 0 ≤ α ≤ π, 0 ≤ β ≤ π and 0 ≤ γ ≤ π

(i) The angles α, β and γ are called the direction angles of the vector underliner.

(ii) The numbers cos α, cos β and cos γ are called direction cosines of the vector underline_r.

--- PAGE 265 ---

Unit 14 Vectors in Space
Mathematics 11
Page 265

Important Result:
Prove that cos²α + cos²β + cos²γ = 1

Proof:
Let
r = [x, y, z] = xi + yj + zk
∴ |r| = √(x² + y² + z²) = r
then r/|r| = [x/r, y/r, z/r] is the unit vector in the direction of the vector r = OP

It can be visualized that the triangle OAP is a right triangle with m∠A = 90°.
Therefore, in right triangle OAP,
cos α = OA/OP = x/r, similarly
cos β = y/r, cos γ = z/r

The numbers cos α = x/r, cos β = y/r and cos γ = z/r are called the direction cosines of OP
cos²α + cos²β + cos²γ = x²/r² + y²/r² + z²/r² = x² + y² + z²/r² = r²/r² = 1

Product of Two Vectors: Multiplication of two vectors is an important algebraic operation in vector algebra. This algebraic operation plays a fundamental role in understanding various physical and mathematical real-life situations. Unlike the multiplication of numbers, the product of vectors can be performed in two distinct ways. The two primary types of vector multiplication are the dot product and the cross product. The dot product is a scalar number, while the cross product is a vector quantity.

14.2 Dot or Scalar Product

14.2.1 Dot or Scalar Product of Two Vectors and Its Geometrical Interpretation

We shall now consider products of two vectors that originated in the study of physics and engineering. The concept of angle between two vectors is expressed in terms of a scalar product of two vectors.

--- PAGE 267 ---

Unit 14 Vectors in Space
Mathematics 11

Definition 1: Let two non-zero vectors underline_u and underline_v, in the plane or in space, have the same initial point. The dot product of underline_u and underline_v, written as underline_u · underline_v, is defined by

underline_u · underline_v = |underline_u| |underline_v| cos θ

Where θ is the angle between underline_u and underline_v and 0 ≤ θ ≤ π

Definition 2:
(a) If underline_u = a₁ î + b₁ ĵ and underline_v = a₂ î + b₂ ĵ are two non-zero vectors in the plane. The dot product underline_u · underline_v is defined by:

underline_u · underline_v = a₁ a₂ + b₁ b₂

(b) If underline_u = a₁ î + b₁ ĵ + c₁ k̂ and underline_v = a₂ î + b₂ ĵ + c₂ k̂ are two non-zero vectors in space. The dot product underline_u · underline_v is defined by

underline_u · underline_v = a₁ a₂ + b₁ b₂ + c₁ c₂


Note: The dot product is also referred as the scalar product or the inner product.

Example 5 Prove the equivalence of above two definitions of dot product of two vectors:
(i) If underline_v = [x₁, y₁] and underlinew = [x₂, y₂] are two vectors in the plane, then underline_v · underline_w = x₁ x₂ + y₁ y₂
(ii) If underline_v and underline_w are two non-zero vectors in the plane, then underline_v · underline_w = |underline_v| |underline_w| cos θ, where θ is the angle between underline_v and underline_w and 0 ≤ θ ≤ π.

Proof: Let underline_v and underline_w be the sides of a triangle then the third side opposite to the angle θ, has length |underline_v - underline_w|. By law of cosines,

|underline_v - underline_w|² = |underline_v|² + |underline_w|² - 2 |underline_v| |underline_w| cos θ

if underline_v = [x₁, y₁] and underline_w = [x₂, y₂], then

underline_v - underline_w = [x₁ - x₂, y₁ - y₂]

So, equation (1) becomes:

(x₁ - x₂)² + (y₁ - y₂)² = x₁² + y₁² + x₂² + y₂² - 2 |underline_v| |underline_w| cos θ


⇒ x₁ x₂ + y₁ y₂ = |underline_v| |underline_w| cos θ = underline_v · underline_w

--- PAGE 268 ---

Unit 14 Vectors in Space
Mathematics 11

14.2.2 Deduction of the Important Results
By applying the definition of dot product to unit vectors  î ,  ĵ  and  k̂ , we have

(a)  î · î = |î| |î| cos 0° = 1

 ĵ · ĵ = |ĵ| |ĵ| cos 0° = 1

 k̂ · k̂ = |k̂| |k̂| cos 0° = 1

(b)  î · ĵ = |î| |ĵ| cos 90° = 0

 ĵ · k̂ = |ĵ| |k̂| cos 90° = 0

 k̂ · î = |k̂| |î| cos 90° = 0

14.2.3 Projection of a Vector along Another Vector
In many physical applications, it is required to know "how much" of a vector is applied along a given direction. For this purpose, we find the projection of one vector along the other vector.

Let ⃗OA = underline_u  and  ⃗OB = underline_v .

Let  θ  be the angle between them, such that  0 ≤ θ ≤ π .

Draw  overline_(BM) ⊥ overline_(OA) . Then  overline_(OM)  is called the projection of  underline_v  along  underline_u .

From the figure:  (overline_(OM))/(overline_(OB)) = cos θ , that is,

 overline_(OM) = |overline_(OB)| cos θ = |underline_v| |cos θ|

Now,  underline_u · underline_v = |underline_u| |underline_v| cos θ = |underline_u| (|underline_v| cos θ) = |underline_u| (overline_(OM))

 = (magnitude of underline_u). (projection of underline_v along underline_u)

Thus, geometrically, the dot product of two vectors represents the product of the magnitude of one vector and the projection of the other vector onto it. In other words, the dot product of two vectors shows how much one vector extends in the direction of another.

Now, by definition,

 cos θ = (underline_u · underline_v)/(|underline_u| |underline_v|)

From (1) and (2),

 overline_(OM) = |underline_v| · (underline_u · underline_v)/(|underline_u| |underline_v|) = (underline_u · underline_v)/(|underline_u|)

--- PAGE 269 ---

Unit 14 Vectors in Space
Mathematics 11

Projection of  underline_v  along  underline_u  =  (underline_u · underline_v)/(|underline_u|)

Similarly, projection of  underline_u  along  underline_v  =  (underline_u · underline_v)/(|underline_v|)

### Properties of Dot Product
Let  underline_u ,  underline_v  and  underline_w  be vectors and let  c  be any real number, then
(i)  underline_u · underline_v = 0 ⇒ underline_u = 0  or  underline_v = 0  or  underline_u ⊥ underline_v
(ii)  underline_u · underline_v = underline_v · underline_u  (Commutative property)
(iii)  underline_u · (underline_v + underline_new) =underline_u · underline_v + underline_u · underline_new (Distributive property)
(iv)  (cunderlineu) · underline_v = c(underlineu · underline_v)  (c is scalar)
(v)  underline_u · underline_u = |underline_u|²

### Dot Product of Vectors in terms of their components
Let  underlineu = a₁ i + b₁ j + c₁ k  and  underlinev = a₂ i + b₂ j + c₂ k  be two non-zero vectors. From distributive law we can write:
 ∴    underlineu · underlinev = (a₁ i + b₁ j + c₁ k) · (a₂ i + b₂ j + c₂ k)
 = a₁ a₂ (i · i) + a₁ b₂ (i · j) + a₁ c₂ (i · k) + b₁ a₂ (j · i) + b₁ b₂ (j · j) + b₁ c₂ (j · k)
 + c₁ a₂ (k · i) + c₁ b₂ (k · j) + c₁ c₂ (k · k)
 ⇒ underlineu · underlinev = a₁ a₂ + b₁ b₂ + c₁ c₂
 ∵    i · i = j · j = k · k = 1
 i · j = j · k = k · i = 0

Hence the dot product of two vectors is the sum of the product of their corresponding components.

### Example 6
Show that the components of a vector are the projections of that vector along  i ,  j  and  k  respectively.

Proof:
Let  underlinev = a i + b j + c k , then
Projection of  underlinev  along  i  =  (underlinev · i)/(|i|) = (a i + b j + c k) · i = a
Projection of  underlinev  along  j  =  (underlinev · j)/(|j|) = (a i + b j + c k) · j = b
Projection of  underlinev  along  k  =  (underlinev · k)/(|k|) = (a i + b j + c k) · k = c

Hence components  a ,  b  and  c  of vector  underlinev = a i + b j + c k  are projections of vector  underline_v  along  i ,  j  and  k  respectively.
